========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F50.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the big thing that got me to finally come in is my eating has gotten really off. Like, I’ll get up for a shift and I’ll think I’m going to eat something normal, but then it’s just coffee and maybe a granola bar, and that’s it for hours. Some days that’s almost all I can get down before work, and I know that’s not really enough, especially doing support work where I’m on my feet and moving around.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m trying to diet or lose weight or anything. That’s kind of what worries me. My scrubs have been feeling loose, like hanging off me more than they should, and I keep noticing it when I’m getting ready. I don’t know, it just feels like my appetite is kind of disappearing, and it’s starting to seem like more than just being busy or skipping a meal here and there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, that’s the thing — I’m not trying to lose weight. I haven’t been dieting or cutting back on purpose or anything like that. It’s more like food just isn’t happening the way it normally would.\n\nLike before a shift, I’ll mean to eat something decent, and then I end up with just coffee and maybe a granola bar, and that’ll be it for hours. And then I’ll notice my scrubs are looser, kind of hanging on me more than they used to, and it bothers me because I didn’t set out for that to happen. It feels like my appetite just sort of dropped off, not like I made a decision to lose weight.\n\nDoctor: How much weight do you think you've lost?\n\nPatient: I’m not exactly sure, honestly. I haven’t stepped on a scale and tracked it or anything, so I don’t want to give you a number that’s made up. But I can tell it’s something, because my scrubs have been fitting differently — like they’re looser around me and kind of hanging off in a way they didn’t before.\n\nIt’s noticeable enough that I keep thinking about it when I’m getting dressed for work. And with how little I’m managing to eat some days — like just coffee and maybe a granola bar before a shift and then nothing for hours — it makes me think I’ve probably lost weight, I just don’t know how much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in the way your clothes fit besides your scrubs?\n\nPatient: Mostly the scrubs are what I notice because I’m in them so much for work, so that’s the clearest thing. But yeah, I guess with some of my regular clothes too, it’s not like I measured it or anything, but waistbands feel a little easier, shirts don’t feel as filled out. Nothing dramatic like I had to buy a whole new wardrobe, but enough that I notice it when I’m getting dressed.\n\nAnd I think it stands out because I’m not trying to change my weight. It’s not like I decided to eat less. It’s more like I’ll start the day thinking I’ll have breakfast before a shift, and then all I manage is coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then hours go by. So when clothes start feeling looser on top of that, it kind of makes me uneasy, like, okay, something’s off here.\n\nDoctor: Are you pleased about the weight loss you've experienced?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know some people might be happy if their clothes got looser, but that’s not how it feels to me. It makes me uneasy, honestly, because I’m not trying to lose weight. I didn’t decide to diet or cut back or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like my eating has gotten messed up — like before work, I’ll think, “Okay, I should eat something,” and then all I manage is coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m on shift and hours go by. And then I notice my scrubs hanging looser, and it just kind of hits me that something’s off. So no, I’m not pleased about it. It feels more worrying than good.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried about the weight loss you've experienced?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do worry about it. It’s not like I’m panicking every second, but it sits in the back of my mind because I’m not trying to lose weight. That’s the part that bothers me. If I had changed my diet on purpose or started exercising more, maybe I’d understand it, but this just feels like my eating has gotten away from me.\n\nSome days before a shift, I’ll mean to eat something real, and then it ends up being just coffee and maybe a granola bar if I can even get that down. Then I’m at work for hours and I realize I haven’t eaten anything else. And when my scrubs start hanging loose on me, it’s hard not to think, “Okay, this isn’t normal for me.”\n\nSo yes, I’m worried. Not pleased, not proud of it — more like uncomfortable and concerned that I’m letting myself run on almost nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in interest in the things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually been one of the bigger things I’ve noticed, even more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nI used to run in Van Cortlandt Park pretty regularly — not like I was training for anything, but it was something that cleared my head. I’d go through the trails, sometimes stop and take pictures if the light was good or there was something interesting. I used to really like photography, just wandering around and noticing things. And I’d play chess with a couple of friends too, not super serious, but we’d meet up or play online and joke around.\n\nNow it’s like… I think about doing those things, but then this feeling comes in like, “What’s the point?” Like running feels pointless, taking photos feels pointless, even texting someone to play chess feels like too much effort for something I won’t enjoy anyway. So instead I end up sitting at home, scrolling on my phone, or checking my budget spreadsheet over and over even when nothing has changed. It’s not enjoyable, exactly. It’s just what I end up doing because it takes less out of me.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds small, but those things used to be part of how I felt like myself. Lately I just kind of let them slide.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, I think. Especially in the mornings. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling there right away, like the day is already ruined before I’ve even done anything — before I’ve even gotten dressed or put on my badge.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m crying or anything dramatic like that. It’s more this flat, sinking feeling, like I’m already behind somehow, or like I’m carrying something I can’t put down. I’ll just lie there for a minute and think, “Okay, I have to get through another day,” and that thought feels heavier than it should.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’ve been feeling sad or down a lot. It’s kind of there in the background most mornings, and then I just try to push through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact percentage, honestly. I haven’t sat down and calculated it like that, or compared numbers carefully enough to say, “yes, it’s definitely over five percent.”\n\nBut I can tell something has changed. My eating has gotten really off — like some days before work I’ll barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m just going into a shift without having really eaten. And my scrubs are fitting looser now, hanging on me more than they used to, which is what made me start paying attention.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s more than five percent in a month, but I’m losing weight without trying to, and it feels noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, gaining weight isn’t what I’m worried about right now. It’s more the opposite, I guess — or not even “worried” in a body-image way, exactly. It’s that my eating has gotten so off that some days I barely get down coffee and maybe a granola bar before I go into a shift, and then I’m already at work realizing I haven’t really eaten.\n\nAnd my scrubs have been hanging loose, which is… it’s hard not to notice that, you know? I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or avoiding food because I’m afraid of getting bigger. It’s more like food just feels like another thing I’m supposed to deal with, and I don’t have the appetite or the push to make myself do it. So, no, I wouldn’t say I worry about gaining weight. The change that’s been bothering me is that I’m eating less and losing weight without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight loss is a good thing because it reduces your chance of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think of it that way. It doesn’t feel like a good thing, and it’s not like I’m relieved because I’m less likely to gain weight or become fat. That’s not really where my head is with it.\n\nIt’s more that my eating has just gotten really off. Like before a shift, some days I barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already going in feeling like I didn’t really take care of myself. And then I notice my scrubs hanging looser on me, and it kind of bothers me because I’m not trying to lose weight. It doesn’t feel intentional or positive. It feels like something is slipping, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything wrong with your body shape or weight?\n\nPatient: No, not in the sense of thinking my body shape is wrong or that my weight is, like… morally bad or something. I don’t look in the mirror and think I need to change my shape, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWhat’s bothering me is more that my eating has gotten so messed up. Like, I’ll be getting ready for work and realize I’m basically running on coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m going into a whole shift like that. And then I notice my scrubs are hanging looser than they used to, and it kind of makes me feel uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. It doesn’t feel like I’m controlling it on purpose or chasing a certain body. It feels more like I’m not taking care of myself the way I normally would. So I wouldn’t say I think there’s something wrong with my body shape or weight — I think something’s wrong with how little I’ve been eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be eating more normally if you weren't feeling so down and sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can’t say it would be perfect, but I do think if I weren’t feeling so down, I’d probably be eating more normally than I am right now.\n\nBecause this doesn’t feel like some intentional change or some decision I made. It’s more like food just drops off the list until I’m already halfway into the day. Like I’ll be getting ready for a shift, and I’ll have coffee because that’s automatic, and maybe I’ll grab a granola bar if it’s sitting there, but then that’s it. And then I’m at work realizing, “Okay, that’s basically all I’ve had,” and by then I’m just pushing through.\n\nAnd the weight loss part… like I said, my scrubs are hanging loose, and I notice it. It doesn’t feel good or like an accomplishment. It feels like a sign that I’m not taking care of myself the way I usually would. So, yeah, I think if my mood wasn’t so low, I’d probably have more appetite, or at least more ability to make myself eat regular meals. Right now it just feels harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're not worthy of eating or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t usually say it that directly to myself, like “I’m not worthy of eating,” but… there is something close to that, I think.\n\nIt’s more like I get this feeling that I’m a burden. Like at the clinic, if I’m slower than I should be or I mess up something small, even something like a room turnover taking longer than it should, I’ll just keep replaying it in my head like I’ve ruined everyone’s whole day. Like now the provider is behind, the next patient is waiting, the nurses are annoyed, and somehow it’s all because I didn’t move fast enough. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them it’s not that big of a deal, things happen, but when it’s me it feels… heavier than that. Like I should’ve done better, and everyone would be better off if I wasn’t slowing things down.\n\nAnd at home it can feel similar. I start thinking my family has to deal with me being like this, and that I’m just adding stress instead of helping. So when it comes to eating or taking care of myself, it’s not always that I consciously think I don’t deserve it, but I do sort of put myself last. Like, “Why should I sit down and make myself food when I’m already causing problems?” That kind of thing. It sounds harsh when I say it out loud, but that’s kind of how it feels in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating a normal meal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I eat a normal meal and immediately think, “I did something wrong by eating,” exactly. It’s more tangled up than that.\n\nIf I actually sit down and eat a real meal, part of me knows that’s probably what I’m supposed to be doing. But then there’s this other part that starts in on me, like… “Why are you taking time for yourself when you’re already making things harder for people?” And I know that sounds kind of unreasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nA lot of it is this guilt I carry around already. Like at the clinic, if I do one small thing wrong or I’m a little slow — even something like turning over a room later than I should — I’ll replay it over and over like I’ve thrown off the whole day for everyone. Like the provider is behind now, the next patient is waiting, the nurses are frustrated, and somehow I caused all of that. So then when I eat, especially if it feels like I’m stopping to take care of myself, I can feel guilty because I’m thinking I don’t deserve to just sit there and have a normal meal when I’ve been a burden.\n\nAnd it’s similar with my family. I think about how they have to deal with me being like this, and then eating can feel… I don’t know, selfish somehow. I know logically people have to eat. I know I’d never tell someone else they should feel guilty for eating dinner. But when it’s me, it gets mixed in with feeling like I’m causing problems for everyone. So yes, I do feel guilty sometimes after eating normally, but it’s less about the food itself and more about feeling like I don’t deserve care when I’m already letting people down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would change if you didn't have any concerns about your weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about my weight, honestly. I mean, I notice the weight loss because my scrubs are hanging loose now, and that kind of makes it hard not to notice, but I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s not like I’m restricting food because I’m worried about gaining or because I’m aiming for some number or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like my eating has just gotten… off. Some days before a shift I’ll barely get down coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already at work and realizing I haven’t really eaten. And it’s not because I’m thinking, “Good, I’m losing weight,” or anything like that. If anything, seeing my clothes fit looser worries me a little, because it feels like proof that I’m not taking care of myself.\n\nSo if I had no concerns about my weight, I don’t know that it would change much, because the weight concern isn’t what’s driving it. The problem is more that I’m just not eating normally, and it’s been bad enough that I’m noticing it physically.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F32.0", "F50.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or avoiding food because I’m afraid of getting bigger. It’s more like food just feels like another thing I’m supposed to deal with, and I don’t have the appetite or the push to make myself do it. So, no, I wouldn’t say I worry about gaining weight. The change that’s been bothering me is that I’m eating less and losing weight without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight loss is a good thing because it reduces your chance of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think of it that way. It doesn’t feel like a good thing, and it’s not like I’m relieved because I’m less likely to gain weight or become fat. That’s not really where my head is with it.\n\nIt’s more that my eating has just gotten really off. Like before a shift, some days I barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already going in feeling like I didn’t really take care of myself. And then I notice my scrubs hanging looser on me, and it kind of bothers me because I’m not trying to lose weight. It doesn’t feel intentional or positive. It feels like something is slipping, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything wrong with your body shape or weight?\n\nPatient: No, not in the sense of thinking my body shape is wrong or that my weight is, like… morally bad or something. I don’t look in the mirror and think I need to change my shape, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWhat’s bothering me is more that my eating has gotten so messed up. Like, I’ll be getting ready for work and realize I’m basically running on coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m going into a whole shift like that. And then I notice my scrubs are hanging looser than they used to, and it kind of makes me feel uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. It doesn’t feel like I’m controlling it on purpose or chasing a certain body. It feels more like I’m not taking care of myself the way I normally would. So I wouldn’t say I think there’s something wrong with my body shape or weight — I think something’s wrong with how little I’ve been eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be eating more normally if you weren't feeling so down and sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can’t say it would be perfect, but I do think if I weren’t feeling so down, I’d probably be eating more normally than I am right now.\n\nBecause this doesn’t feel like some intentional change or some decision I made. It’s more like food just drops off the list until I’m already halfway into the day. Like I’ll be getting ready for a shift, and I’ll have coffee because that’s automatic, and maybe I’ll grab a granola bar if it’s sitting there, but then that’s it. And then I’m at work realizing, “Okay, that’s basically all I’ve had,” and by then I’m just pushing through.\n\nAnd the weight loss part… like I said, my scrubs are hanging loose, and I notice it. It doesn’t feel good or like an accomplishment. It feels like a sign that I’m not taking care of myself the way I usually would. So, yeah, I think if my mood wasn’t so low, I’d probably have more appetite, or at least more ability to make myself eat regular meals. Right now it just feels harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're not worthy of eating or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t usually say it that directly to myself, like “I’m not worthy of eating,” but… there is something close to that, I think.\n\nIt’s more like I get this feeling that I’m a burden. Like at the clinic, if I’m slower than I should be or I mess up something small, even something like a room turnover taking longer than it should, I’ll just keep replaying it in my head like I’ve ruined everyone’s whole day. Like now the provider is behind, the next patient is waiting, the nurses are annoyed, and somehow it’s all because I didn’t move fast enough. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them it’s not that big of a deal, things happen, but when it’s me it feels… heavier than that. Like I should’ve done better, and everyone would be better off if I wasn’t slowing things down.\n\nAnd at home it can feel similar. I start thinking my family has to deal with me being like this, and that I’m just adding stress instead of helping. So when it comes to eating or taking care of myself, it’s not always that I consciously think I don’t deserve it, but I do sort of put myself last. Like, “Why should I sit down and make myself food when I’m already causing problems?” That kind of thing. It sounds harsh when I say it out loud, but that’s kind of how it feels in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating a normal meal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I eat a normal meal and immediately think, “I did something wrong by eating,” exactly. It’s more tangled up than that.\n\nIf I actually sit down and eat a real meal, part of me knows that’s probably what I’m supposed to be doing. But then there’s this other part that starts in on me, like… “Why are you taking time for yourself when you’re already making things harder for people?” And I know that sounds kind of unreasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nA lot of it is this guilt I carry around already. Like at the clinic, if I do one small thing wrong or I’m a little slow — even something like turning over a room later than I should — I’ll replay it over and over like I’ve thrown off the whole day for everyone. Like the provider is behind now, the next patient is waiting, the nurses are frustrated, and somehow I caused all of that. So then when I eat, especially if it feels like I’m stopping to take care of myself, I can feel guilty because I’m thinking I don’t deserve to just sit there and have a normal meal when I’ve been a burden.\n\nAnd it’s similar with my family. I think about how they have to deal with me being like this, and then eating can feel… I don’t know, selfish somehow. I know logically people have to eat. I know I’d never tell someone else they should feel guilty for eating dinner. But when it’s me, it gets mixed in with feeling like I’m causing problems for everyone. So yes, I do feel guilty sometimes after eating normally, but it’s less about the food itself and more about feeling like I don’t deserve care when I’m already letting people down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would change if you didn't have any concerns about your weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about my weight, honestly. I mean, I notice the weight loss because my scrubs are hanging loose now, and that kind of makes it hard not to notice, but I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s not like I’m restricting food because I’m worried about gaining or because I’m aiming for some number or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like my eating has just gotten… off. Some days before a shift I’ll barely get down coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already at work and realizing I haven’t really eaten. And it’s not because I’m thinking, “Good, I’m losing weight,” or anything like that. If anything, seeing my clothes fit looser worries me a little, because it feels like proof that I’m not taking care of myself.\n\nSo if I had no concerns about my weight, I don’t know that it would change much, because the weight concern isn’t what’s driving it. The problem is more that I’m just not eating normally, and it’s been bad enough that I’m noticing it physically.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think fear of gaining weight is really the thing stopping me, no. I mean, if I’m being honest, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I better not eat because I’ll gain weight.” That’s not how it feels.

What’s been worrying me more is that my eating has just gotten so irregular and neglected, I guess. Like before a shift, some days I barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already into the day and realize that’s basically all I’ve had. And then my scrubs have been hanging loose, which I notice because I wear them all the time, and it kind of hits me like, okay, this isn’t just in my head — I’m actually not taking care of myself.

So if I didn’t fear gaining weight… I mean, I don’t really think I do fear it, not in that way. I probably would eat more if the other stuff wasn’t getting in the way, but not because the weight fear was removed. It’s more that I’m not eating enough even though I’m not trying to lose weight.

I haven’t been dieting or cutting back on purpose or anything like that. It’s more like food just isn’t happening the way it normally would.\n\nLike before a shift, I’ll mean to eat something decent, and then I end up with just coffee and maybe a granola bar, and that’ll be it for hours. And then I’ll notice my scrubs are looser, kind of hanging on me more than they used to, and it bothers me because I didn’t set out for that to happen. It feels like my appetite just sort of dropped off, not like I made a decision to lose weight.\n\nDoctor: How much weight do you think you've lost?\n\nPatient: I’m not exactly sure, honestly. I haven’t stepped on a scale and tracked it or anything, so I don’t want to give you a number that’s made up. But I can tell it’s something, because my scrubs have been fitting differently — like they’re looser around me and kind of hanging off in a way they didn’t before.\n\nIt’s noticeable enough that I keep thinking about it when I’m getting dressed for work. And with how little I’m managing to eat some days — like just coffee and maybe a granola bar before a shift and then nothing for hours — it makes me think I’ve probably lost weight, I just don’t know how much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in the way your clothes fit besides your scrubs?\n\nPatient: Mostly the scrubs are what I notice because I’m in them so much for work, so that’s the clearest thing. But yeah, I guess with some of my regular clothes too, it’s not like I measured it or anything, but waistbands feel a little easier, shirts don’t feel as filled out. Nothing dramatic like I had to buy a whole new wardrobe, but enough that I notice it when I’m getting dressed.\n\nAnd I think it stands out because I’m not trying to change my weight. It’s not like I decided to eat less. It’s more like I’ll start the day thinking I’ll have breakfast before a shift, and then all I manage is coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then hours go by. So when clothes start feeling looser on top of that, it kind of makes me uneasy, like, okay, something’s off here.\n\nDoctor: Are you pleased about the weight loss you've experienced?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know some people might be happy if their clothes got looser, but that’s not how it feels to me. It makes me uneasy, honestly, because I’m not trying to lose weight. I didn’t decide to diet or cut back or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like my eating has gotten messed up — like before work, I’ll think, “Okay, I should eat something,” and then all I manage is coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m on shift and hours go by. And then I notice my scrubs hanging looser, and it just kind of hits me that something’s off. So no, I’m not pleased about it. It feels more worrying than good.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried about the weight loss you've experienced?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do worry about it. It’s not like I’m panicking every second, but it sits in the back of my mind because I’m not trying to lose weight. That’s the part that bothers me. If I had changed my diet on purpose or started exercising more, maybe I’d understand it, but this just feels like my eating has gotten away from me.\n\nSome days before a shift, I’ll mean to eat something real, and then it ends up being just coffee and maybe a granola bar if I can even get that down. Then I’m at work for hours and I realize I haven’t eaten anything else. And when my scrubs start hanging loose on me, it’s hard not to think, “Okay, this isn’t normal for me.”\n\nSo yes, I’m worried. Not pleased, not proud of it — more like uncomfortable and concerned that I’m letting myself run on almost nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in interest in the things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually been one of the bigger things I’ve noticed, even more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nI used to run in Van Cortlandt Park pretty regularly — not like I was training for anything, but it was something that cleared my head. I’d go through the trails, sometimes stop and take pictures if the light was good or there was something interesting. I used to really like photography, just wandering around and noticing things. And I’d play chess with a couple of friends too, not super serious, but we’d meet up or play online and joke around.\n\nNow it’s like… I think about doing those things, but then this feeling comes in like, “What’s the point?” Like running feels pointless, taking photos feels pointless, even texting someone to play chess feels like too much effort for something I won’t enjoy anyway. So instead I end up sitting at home, scrolling on my phone, or checking my budget spreadsheet over and over even when nothing has changed. It’s not enjoyable, exactly. It’s just what I end up doing because it takes less out of me.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds small, but those things used to be part of how I felt like myself. Lately I just kind of let them slide.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, I think. Especially in the mornings. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling there right away, like the day is already ruined before I’ve even done anything — before I’ve even gotten dressed or put on my badge.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m crying or anything dramatic like that. It’s more this flat, sinking feeling, like I’m already behind somehow, or like I’m carrying something I can’t put down. I’ll just lie there for a minute and think, “Okay, I have to get through another day,” and that thought feels heavier than it should.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’ve been feeling sad or down a lot. It’s kind of there in the background most mornings, and then I just try to push through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact percentage, honestly. I haven’t sat down and calculated it like that, or compared numbers carefully enough to say, “yes, it’s definitely over five percent.”\n\nBut I can tell something has changed. My eating has gotten really off — like some days before work I’ll barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m just going into a shift without having really eaten. And my scrubs are fitting looser now, hanging on me more than they used to, which is what made me start paying attention.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s more than five percent in a month, but I’m losing weight without trying to, and it feels noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, gaining weight isn’t what I’m worried about right now. It’s more the opposite, I guess — or not even “worried” in a body-image way, exactly. It’s that my eating has gotten so off that some days I barely get down coffee and maybe a granola bar before I go into a shift, and then I’m already at work realizing I haven’t really eaten.\n\nAnd my scrubs have been hanging loose, which is… it’s hard not to notice that, you know? I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or avoiding food because I’m afraid of getting bigger. It’s more like food just feels like another thing I’m supposed to deal with, and I don’t have the appetite or the push to make myself do it. So, no, I wouldn’t say I worry about gaining weight. The change that’s been bothering me is that I’m eating less and losing weight without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight loss is a good thing because it reduces your chance of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think of it that way. It doesn’t feel like a good thing, and it’s not like I’m relieved because I’m less likely to gain weight or become fat. That’s not really where my head is with it.\n\nIt’s more that my eating has just gotten really off. Like before a shift, some days I barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already going in feeling like I didn’t really take care of myself. And then I notice my scrubs hanging looser on me, and it kind of bothers me because I’m not trying to lose weight. It doesn’t feel intentional or positive. It feels like something is slipping, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything wrong with your body shape or weight?\n\nPatient: No, not in the sense of thinking my body shape is wrong or that my weight is, like… morally bad or something. I don’t look in the mirror and think I need to change my shape, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWhat’s bothering me is more that my eating has gotten so messed up. Like, I’ll be getting ready for work and realize I’m basically running on coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m going into a whole shift like that. And then I notice my scrubs are hanging looser than they used to, and it kind of makes me feel uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. It doesn’t feel like I’m controlling it on purpose or chasing a certain body. It feels more like I’m not taking care of myself the way I normally would. So I wouldn’t say I think there’s something wrong with my body shape or weight — I think something’s wrong with how little I’ve been eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be eating more normally if you weren't feeling so down and sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can’t say it would be perfect, but I do think if I weren’t feeling so down, I’d probably be eating more normally than I am right now.\n\nBecause this doesn’t feel like some intentional change or some decision I made. It’s more like food just drops off the list until I’m already halfway into the day. Like I’ll be getting ready for a shift, and I’ll have coffee because that’s automatic, and maybe I’ll grab a granola bar if it’s sitting there, but then that’s it. And then I’m at work realizing, “Okay, that’s basically all I’ve had,” and by then I’m just pushing through.\n\nAnd the weight loss part… like I said, my scrubs are hanging loose, and I notice it. It doesn’t feel good or like an accomplishment. It feels like a sign that I’m not taking care of myself the way I usually would. So, yeah, I think if my mood wasn’t so low, I’d probably have more appetite, or at least more ability to make myself eat regular meals. Right now it just feels harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're not worthy of eating or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t usually say it that directly to myself, like “I’m not worthy of eating,” but… there is something close to that, I think.\n\nIt’s more like I get this feeling that I’m a burden. Like at the clinic, if I’m slower than I should be or I mess up something small, even something like a room turnover taking longer than it should, I’ll just keep replaying it in my head like I’ve ruined everyone’s whole day. Like now the provider is behind, the next patient is waiting, the nurses are annoyed, and somehow it’s all because I didn’t move fast enough. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them it’s not that big of a deal, things happen, but when it’s me it feels… heavier than that. Like I should’ve done better, and everyone would be better off if I wasn’t slowing things down.\n\nAnd at home it can feel similar. I start thinking my family has to deal with me being like this, and that I’m just adding stress instead of helping. So when it comes to eating or taking care of myself, it’s not always that I consciously think I don’t deserve it, but I do sort of put myself last. Like, “Why should I sit down and make myself food when I’m already causing problems?” That kind of thing. It sounds harsh when I say it out loud, but that’s kind of how it feels in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating a normal meal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I eat a normal meal and immediately think, “I did something wrong by eating,” exactly. It’s more tangled up than that.\n\nIf I actually sit down and eat a real meal, part of me knows that’s probably what I’m supposed to be doing. But then there’s this other part that starts in on me, like… “Why are you taking time for yourself when you’re already making things harder for people?” And I know that sounds kind of unreasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nA lot of it is this guilt I carry around already. Like at the clinic, if I do one small thing wrong or I’m a little slow — even something like turning over a room later than I should — I’ll replay it over and over like I’ve thrown off the whole day for everyone. Like the provider is behind now, the next patient is waiting, the nurses are frustrated, and somehow I caused all of that. So then when I eat, especially if it feels like I’m stopping to take care of myself, I can feel guilty because I’m thinking I don’t deserve to just sit there and have a normal meal when I’ve been a burden.\n\nAnd it’s similar with my family. I think about how they have to deal with me being like this, and then eating can feel… I don’t know, selfish somehow. I know logically people have to eat. I know I’d never tell someone else they should feel guilty for eating dinner. But when it’s me, it gets mixed in with feeling like I’m causing problems for everyone. So yes, I do feel guilty sometimes after eating normally, but it’s less about the food itself and more about feeling like I don’t deserve care when I’m already letting people down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would change if you didn't have any concerns about your weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about my weight, honestly. I mean, I notice the weight loss because my scrubs are hanging loose now, and that kind of makes it hard not to notice, but I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s not like I’m restricting food because I’m worried about gaining or because I’m aiming for some number or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like my eating has just gotten… off. Some days before a shift I’ll barely get down coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already at work and realizing I haven’t really eaten. And it’s not because I’m thinking, “Good, I’m losing weight,” or anything like that. If anything, seeing my clothes fit looser worries me a little, because it feels like proof that I’m not taking care of myself.\n\nSo if I had no concerns about my weight, I don’t know that it would change much, because the weight concern isn’t what’s driving it. The problem is more that I’m just not eating normally, and it’s been bad enough that I’m noticing it physically.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think fear of gaining weight is really the thing stopping me, no. I mean, if I’m being honest, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I better not eat because I’ll gain weight.” That’s not how it feels.\n\nWhat’s been worrying me more is that my eating has just gotten so irregular and neglected, I guess. Like before a shift, some days I barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already into the day and realize that’s basically all I’ve had. And then my scrubs have been hanging loose, which I notice because I wear them all the time, and it kind of hits me like, okay, this isn’t just in my head — I’m actually not taking care of myself.\n\nSo if I didn’t fear gaining weight… I mean, I don’t really think I do fear it, not in that way. I probably would eat more if the other stuff wasn’t getting in the way, but not because the weight fear was removed. It’s more that I’m not eating enough even though I’m not trying to lose weight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in the way people usually mean that, I don’t think. I mean, I’m aware of my body because my scrubs have been hanging loose, and I notice that when I’m getting dressed for work or moving around during a shift. But it’s not like I’m looking in the mirror and thinking I need to be smaller or that I shouldn’t eat because of how I look.

It’s more that the loose scrubs kind of make it obvious that my eating has gotten off. Like some mornings before work I barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already into the shift and realize I haven’t really had anything else. And then the clothes fitting differently makes it feel less like “oh, I just skipped breakfast” and more like… okay, this is actually affecting me.

So I guess how I feel about my body affects me in the sense that I’m noticing the weight loss and it worries me a little, but it’s not what’s causing the eating problem. I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s more like I’m neglecting eating, and my body is showing that.

I haven’t been dieting or cutting back on purpose or anything like that. It’s more like food just isn’t happening the way it normally would.\n\nLike before a shift, I’ll mean to eat something decent, and then I end up with just coffee and maybe a granola bar, and that’ll be it for hours. And then I’ll notice my scrubs are looser, kind of hanging on me more than they used to, and it bothers me because I didn’t set out for that to happen. It feels like my appetite just sort of dropped off, not like I made a decision to lose weight.\n\nDoctor: How much weight do you think you've lost?\n\nPatient: I’m not exactly sure, honestly. I haven’t stepped on a scale and tracked it or anything, so I don’t want to give you a number that’s made up. But I can tell it’s something, because my scrubs have been fitting differently — like they’re looser around me and kind of hanging off in a way they didn’t before.\n\nIt’s noticeable enough that I keep thinking about it when I’m getting dressed for work. And with how little I’m managing to eat some days — like just coffee and maybe a granola bar before a shift and then nothing for hours — it makes me think I’ve probably lost weight, I just don’t know how much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in the way your clothes fit besides your scrubs?\n\nPatient: Mostly the scrubs are what I notice because I’m in them so much for work, so that’s the clearest thing. But yeah, I guess with some of my regular clothes too, it’s not like I measured it or anything, but waistbands feel a little easier, shirts don’t feel as filled out. Nothing dramatic like I had to buy a whole new wardrobe, but enough that I notice it when I’m getting dressed.\n\nAnd I think it stands out because I’m not trying to change my weight. It’s not like I decided to eat less. It’s more like I’ll start the day thinking I’ll have breakfast before a shift, and then all I manage is coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then hours go by. So when clothes start feeling looser on top of that, it kind of makes me uneasy, like, okay, something’s off here.\n\nDoctor: Are you pleased about the weight loss you've experienced?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know some people might be happy if their clothes got looser, but that’s not how it feels to me. It makes me uneasy, honestly, because I’m not trying to lose weight. I didn’t decide to diet or cut back or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like my eating has gotten messed up — like before work, I’ll think, “Okay, I should eat something,” and then all I manage is coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m on shift and hours go by. And then I notice my scrubs hanging looser, and it just kind of hits me that something’s off. So no, I’m not pleased about it. It feels more worrying than good.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried about the weight loss you've experienced?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do worry about it. It’s not like I’m panicking every second, but it sits in the back of my mind because I’m not trying to lose weight. That’s the part that bothers me. If I had changed my diet on purpose or started exercising more, maybe I’d understand it, but this just feels like my eating has gotten away from me.\n\nSome days before a shift, I’ll mean to eat something real, and then it ends up being just coffee and maybe a granola bar if I can even get that down. Then I’m at work for hours and I realize I haven’t eaten anything else. And when my scrubs start hanging loose on me, it’s hard not to think, “Okay, this isn’t normal for me.”\n\nSo yes, I’m worried. Not pleased, not proud of it — more like uncomfortable and concerned that I’m letting myself run on almost nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in interest in the things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually been one of the bigger things I’ve noticed, even more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nI used to run in Van Cortlandt Park pretty regularly — not like I was training for anything, but it was something that cleared my head. I’d go through the trails, sometimes stop and take pictures if the light was good or there was something interesting. I used to really like photography, just wandering around and noticing things. And I’d play chess with a couple of friends too, not super serious, but we’d meet up or play online and joke around.\n\nNow it’s like… I think about doing those things, but then this feeling comes in like, “What’s the point?” Like running feels pointless, taking photos feels pointless, even texting someone to play chess feels like too much effort for something I won’t enjoy anyway. So instead I end up sitting at home, scrolling on my phone, or checking my budget spreadsheet over and over even when nothing has changed. It’s not enjoyable, exactly. It’s just what I end up doing because it takes less out of me.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds small, but those things used to be part of how I felt like myself. Lately I just kind of let them slide.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, I think. Especially in the mornings. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling there right away, like the day is already ruined before I’ve even done anything — before I’ve even gotten dressed or put on my badge.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m crying or anything dramatic like that. It’s more this flat, sinking feeling, like I’m already behind somehow, or like I’m carrying something I can’t put down. I’ll just lie there for a minute and think, “Okay, I have to get through another day,” and that thought feels heavier than it should.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’ve been feeling sad or down a lot. It’s kind of there in the background most mornings, and then I just try to push through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact percentage, honestly. I haven’t sat down and calculated it like that, or compared numbers carefully enough to say, “yes, it’s definitely over five percent.”\n\nBut I can tell something has changed. My eating has gotten really off — like some days before work I’ll barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m just going into a shift without having really eaten. And my scrubs are fitting looser now, hanging on me more than they used to, which is what made me start paying attention.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s more than five percent in a month, but I’m losing weight without trying to, and it feels noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, gaining weight isn’t what I’m worried about right now. It’s more the opposite, I guess — or not even “worried” in a body-image way, exactly. It’s that my eating has gotten so off that some days I barely get down coffee and maybe a granola bar before I go into a shift, and then I’m already at work realizing I haven’t really eaten.\n\nAnd my scrubs have been hanging loose, which is… it’s hard not to notice that, you know? I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or avoiding food because I’m afraid of getting bigger. It’s more like food just feels like another thing I’m supposed to deal with, and I don’t have the appetite or the push to make myself do it. So, no, I wouldn’t say I worry about gaining weight. The change that’s been bothering me is that I’m eating less and losing weight without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight loss is a good thing because it reduces your chance of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think of it that way. It doesn’t feel like a good thing, and it’s not like I’m relieved because I’m less likely to gain weight or become fat. That’s not really where my head is with it.\n\nIt’s more that my eating has just gotten really off. Like before a shift, some days I barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already going in feeling like I didn’t really take care of myself. And then I notice my scrubs hanging looser on me, and it kind of bothers me because I’m not trying to lose weight. It doesn’t feel intentional or positive. It feels like something is slipping, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything wrong with your body shape or weight?\n\nPatient: No, not in the sense of thinking my body shape is wrong or that my weight is, like… morally bad or something. I don’t look in the mirror and think I need to change my shape, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWhat’s bothering me is more that my eating has gotten so messed up. Like, I’ll be getting ready for work and realize I’m basically running on coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m going into a whole shift like that. And then I notice my scrubs are hanging looser than they used to, and it kind of makes me feel uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. It doesn’t feel like I’m controlling it on purpose or chasing a certain body. It feels more like I’m not taking care of myself the way I normally would. So I wouldn’t say I think there’s something wrong with my body shape or weight — I think something’s wrong with how little I’ve been eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be eating more normally if you weren't feeling so down and sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can’t say it would be perfect, but I do think if I weren’t feeling so down, I’d probably be eating more normally than I am right now.\n\nBecause this doesn’t feel like some intentional change or some decision I made. It’s more like food just drops off the list until I’m already halfway into the day. Like I’ll be getting ready for a shift, and I’ll have coffee because that’s automatic, and maybe I’ll grab a granola bar if it’s sitting there, but then that’s it. And then I’m at work realizing, “Okay, that’s basically all I’ve had,” and by then I’m just pushing through.\n\nAnd the weight loss part… like I said, my scrubs are hanging loose, and I notice it. It doesn’t feel good or like an accomplishment. It feels like a sign that I’m not taking care of myself the way I usually would. So, yeah, I think if my mood wasn’t so low, I’d probably have more appetite, or at least more ability to make myself eat regular meals. Right now it just feels harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're not worthy of eating or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t usually say it that directly to myself, like “I’m not worthy of eating,” but… there is something close to that, I think.\n\nIt’s more like I get this feeling that I’m a burden. Like at the clinic, if I’m slower than I should be or I mess up something small, even something like a room turnover taking longer than it should, I’ll just keep replaying it in my head like I’ve ruined everyone’s whole day. Like now the provider is behind, the next patient is waiting, the nurses are annoyed, and somehow it’s all because I didn’t move fast enough. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them it’s not that big of a deal, things happen, but when it’s me it feels… heavier than that. Like I should’ve done better, and everyone would be better off if I wasn’t slowing things down.\n\nAnd at home it can feel similar. I start thinking my family has to deal with me being like this, and that I’m just adding stress instead of helping. So when it comes to eating or taking care of myself, it’s not always that I consciously think I don’t deserve it, but I do sort of put myself last. Like, “Why should I sit down and make myself food when I’m already causing problems?” That kind of thing. It sounds harsh when I say it out loud, but that’s kind of how it feels in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating a normal meal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I eat a normal meal and immediately think, “I did something wrong by eating,” exactly. It’s more tangled up than that.\n\nIf I actually sit down and eat a real meal, part of me knows that’s probably what I’m supposed to be doing. But then there’s this other part that starts in on me, like… “Why are you taking time for yourself when you’re already making things harder for people?” And I know that sounds kind of unreasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nA lot of it is this guilt I carry around already. Like at the clinic, if I do one small thing wrong or I’m a little slow — even something like turning over a room later than I should — I’ll replay it over and over like I’ve thrown off the whole day for everyone. Like the provider is behind now, the next patient is waiting, the nurses are frustrated, and somehow I caused all of that. So then when I eat, especially if it feels like I’m stopping to take care of myself, I can feel guilty because I’m thinking I don’t deserve to just sit there and have a normal meal when I’ve been a burden.\n\nAnd it’s similar with my family. I think about how they have to deal with me being like this, and then eating can feel… I don’t know, selfish somehow. I know logically people have to eat. I know I’d never tell someone else they should feel guilty for eating dinner. But when it’s me, it gets mixed in with feeling like I’m causing problems for everyone. So yes, I do feel guilty sometimes after eating normally, but it’s less about the food itself and more about feeling like I don’t deserve care when I’m already letting people down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would change if you didn't have any concerns about your weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about my weight, honestly. I mean, I notice the weight loss because my scrubs are hanging loose now, and that kind of makes it hard not to notice, but I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s not like I’m restricting food because I’m worried about gaining or because I’m aiming for some number or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like my eating has just gotten… off. Some days before a shift I’ll barely get down coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already at work and realizing I haven’t really eaten. And it’s not because I’m thinking, “Good, I’m losing weight,” or anything like that. If anything, seeing my clothes fit looser worries me a little, because it feels like proof that I’m not taking care of myself.\n\nSo if I had no concerns about my weight, I don’t know that it would change much, because the weight concern isn’t what’s driving it. The problem is more that I’m just not eating normally, and it’s been bad enough that I’m noticing it physically.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think fear of gaining weight is really the thing stopping me, no. I mean, if I’m being honest, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I better not eat because I’ll gain weight.” That’s not how it feels.\n\nWhat’s been worrying me more is that my eating has just gotten so irregular and neglected, I guess. Like before a shift, some days I barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already into the day and realize that’s basically all I’ve had. And then my scrubs have been hanging loose, which I notice because I wear them all the time, and it kind of hits me like, okay, this isn’t just in my head — I’m actually not taking care of myself.\n\nSo if I didn’t fear gaining weight… I mean, I don’t really think I do fear it, not in that way. I probably would eat more if the other stuff wasn’t getting in the way, but not because the weight fear was removed. It’s more that I’m not eating enough even though I’m not trying to lose weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are affected by how you feel about your body?\n\nPatient: Not really in the way people usually mean that, I don’t think. I mean, I’m aware of my body because my scrubs have been hanging loose, and I notice that when I’m getting dressed for work or moving around during a shift. But it’s not like I’m looking in the mirror and thinking I need to be smaller or that I shouldn’t eat because of how I look.\n\nIt’s more that the loose scrubs kind of make it obvious that my eating has gotten off. Like some mornings before work I barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already into the shift and realize I haven’t really had anything else. And then the clothes fitting differently makes it feel less like “oh, I just skipped breakfast” and more like… okay, this is actually affecting me.\n\nSo I guess how I feel about my body affects me in the sense that I’m noticing the weight loss and it worries me a little, but it’s not what’s causing the eating problem. I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s more like I’m neglecting eating, and my body is showing that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess I do, sometimes. Not in a dramatic way where I think, “I’m not allowed to eat,” exactly, but when I’m really down, there’s this feeling like I don’t deserve much of anything, including taking care of myself.

I keep getting stuck on the idea that I’m just a burden. At work, at the clinic, even at home with my family. Like if I make some small mistake — say a room turnover takes too long, or I forget something minor and someone else has to wait — I’ll replay it in my head like I ruined everyone’s whole day. I know, logically, it might just be a delay or something that happens in a busy place, but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment. It feels like I’ve let everybody down, like I’m making things harder for people who already have enough to deal with.

And then when I feel like that, eating feels… I don’t know, almost like something I haven’t earned. Like why am I stopping to feed myself when I’ve already caused problems or made people carry me? I’m not saying that makes sense. If someone else told me that, I’d tell them they absolutely deserve to eat. But when it’s me, when I’m sad or ashamed, it’s harder to believe that.

It’s just what I end up doing because it takes less out of me.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds small, but those things used to be part of how I felt like myself. Lately I just kind of let them slide.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, I think. Especially in the mornings. I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling there right away, like the day is already ruined before I’ve even done anything — before I’ve even gotten dressed or put on my badge.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m crying or anything dramatic like that. It’s more this flat, sinking feeling, like I’m already behind somehow, or like I’m carrying something I can’t put down. I’ll just lie there for a minute and think, “Okay, I have to get through another day,” and that thought feels heavier than it should.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’ve been feeling sad or down a lot. It’s kind of there in the background most mornings, and then I just try to push through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact percentage, honestly. My eating has gotten really off — like some days before work I’ll barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m just going into a shift without having really eaten. I mean, gaining weight isn’t what I’m worried about right now. It’s more the opposite, I guess — or not even “worried” in a body-image way, exactly. It’s that my eating has gotten so off that some days I barely get down coffee and maybe a granola bar before I go into a shift, and then I’m already at work realizing I haven’t really eaten.\n\nAnd my scrubs have been hanging loose, which is… it’s hard not to notice that, you know? I’m not trying to lose weight. I’m not dieting or avoiding food because I’m afraid of getting bigger. It’s more like food just feels like another thing I’m supposed to deal with, and I don’t have the appetite or the push to make myself do it. So, no, I wouldn’t say I worry about gaining weight. The change that’s been bothering me is that I’m eating less and losing weight without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight loss is a good thing because it reduces your chance of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think of it that way. It doesn’t feel like a good thing, and it’s not like I’m relieved because I’m less likely to gain weight or become fat. That’s not really where my head is with it.\n\nIt’s more that my eating has just gotten really off. Like before a shift, some days I barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already going in feeling like I didn’t really take care of myself. Like, I’ll be getting ready for work and realize I’m basically running on coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m going into a whole shift like that. And then I notice my scrubs are hanging looser than they used to, and it kind of makes me feel uneasy because I’m not trying to lose weight. It doesn’t feel like I’m controlling it on purpose or chasing a certain body. It feels more like I’m not taking care of myself the way I normally would. So I wouldn’t say I think there’s something wrong with my body shape or weight — I think something’s wrong with how little I’ve been eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be eating more normally if you weren't feeling so down and sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can’t say it would be perfect, but I do think if I weren’t feeling so down, I’d probably be eating more normally than I am right now.\n\nBecause this doesn’t feel like some intentional change or some decision I made. It’s more like food just drops off the list until I’m already halfway into the day. Like I’ll be getting ready for a shift, and I’ll have coffee because that’s automatic, and maybe I’ll grab a granola bar if it’s sitting there, but then that’s it. And then I’m at work realizing, “Okay, that’s basically all I’ve had,” and by then I’m just pushing through.\n\nAnd the weight loss part… like I said, my scrubs are hanging loose, and I notice it. It doesn’t feel good or like an accomplishment. It feels like a sign that I’m not taking care of myself the way I usually would. So, yeah, I think if my mood wasn’t so low, I’d probably have more appetite, or at least more ability to make myself eat regular meals. Right now it just feels harder than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're not worthy of eating or taking care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t usually say it that directly to myself, like “I’m not worthy of eating,” but… there is something close to that, I think.\n\nIt’s more like I get this feeling that I’m a burden. Like at the clinic, if I’m slower than I should be or I mess up something small, even something like a room turnover taking longer than it should, I’ll just keep replaying it in my head like I’ve ruined everyone’s whole day. Like now the provider is behind, the next patient is waiting, the nurses are annoyed, and somehow it’s all because I didn’t move fast enough. And I know, if someone else said that to me, I’d probably tell them it’s not that big of a deal, things happen, but when it’s me it feels… heavier than that. Like I should’ve done better, and everyone would be better off if I wasn’t slowing things down.\n\nAnd at home it can feel similar. I start thinking my family has to deal with me being like this, and that I’m just adding stress instead of helping. So when it comes to eating or taking care of myself, it’s not always that I consciously think I don’t deserve it, but I do sort of put myself last. Like, “Why should I sit down and make myself food when I’m already causing problems?” That kind of thing. It sounds harsh when I say it out loud, but that’s kind of how it feels in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating a normal meal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I eat a normal meal and immediately think, “I did something wrong by eating,” exactly. It’s more tangled up than that.\n\nIf I actually sit down and eat a real meal, part of me knows that’s probably what I’m supposed to be doing. But then there’s this other part that starts in on me, like… “Why are you taking time for yourself when you’re already making things harder for people?” And I know that sounds kind of unreasonable, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nA lot of it is this guilt I carry around already. Like at the clinic, if I do one small thing wrong or I’m a little slow — even something like turning over a room later than I should — I’ll replay it over and over like I’ve thrown off the whole day for everyone. Like the provider is behind now, the next patient is waiting, the nurses are frustrated, and somehow I caused all of that. So then when I eat, especially if it feels like I’m stopping to take care of myself, I can feel guilty because I’m thinking I don’t deserve to just sit there and have a normal meal when I’ve been a burden.\n\nAnd it’s similar with my family. I think about how they have to deal with me being like this, and then eating can feel… I don’t know, selfish somehow. I know logically people have to eat. I know I’d never tell someone else they should feel guilty for eating dinner. But when it’s me, it gets mixed in with feeling like I’m causing problems for everyone. So yes, I do feel guilty sometimes after eating normally, but it’s less about the food itself and more about feeling like I don’t deserve care when I’m already letting people down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would change if you didn't have any concerns about your weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really about my weight, honestly. I mean, I notice the weight loss because my scrubs are hanging loose now, and that kind of makes it hard not to notice, but I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s not like I’m restricting food because I’m worried about gaining or because I’m aiming for some number or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like my eating has just gotten… off. Some days before a shift I’ll barely get down coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already at work and realizing I haven’t really eaten. And it’s not because I’m thinking, “Good, I’m losing weight,” or anything like that. If anything, seeing my clothes fit looser worries me a little, because it feels like proof that I’m not taking care of myself.\n\nSo if I had no concerns about my weight, I don’t know that it would change much, because the weight concern isn’t what’s driving it. The problem is more that I’m just not eating normally, and it’s been bad enough that I’m noticing it physically.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't fear gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think fear of gaining weight is really the thing stopping me, no. I mean, if I’m being honest, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I better not eat because I’ll gain weight.” That’s not how it feels.\n\nWhat’s been worrying me more is that my eating has just gotten so irregular and neglected, I guess. Like before a shift, some days I barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already into the day and realize that’s basically all I’ve had. And then my scrubs have been hanging loose, which I notice because I wear them all the time, and it kind of hits me like, okay, this isn’t just in my head — I’m actually not taking care of myself.\n\nSo if I didn’t fear gaining weight… I mean, I don’t really think I do fear it, not in that way. I probably would eat more if the other stuff wasn’t getting in the way, but not because the weight fear was removed. It’s more that I’m not eating enough even though I’m not trying to lose weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are affected by how you feel about your body?\n\nPatient: Not really in the way people usually mean that, I don’t think. I mean, I’m aware of my body because my scrubs have been hanging loose, and I notice that when I’m getting dressed for work or moving around during a shift. But it’s not like I’m looking in the mirror and thinking I need to be smaller or that I shouldn’t eat because of how I look.\n\nIt’s more that the loose scrubs kind of make it obvious that my eating has gotten off. Like some mornings before work I barely manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already into the shift and realize I haven’t really had anything else. And then the clothes fitting differently makes it feel less like “oh, I just skipped breakfast” and more like… okay, this is actually affecting me.\n\nSo I guess how I feel about my body affects me in the sense that I’m noticing the weight loss and it worries me a little, but it’s not what’s causing the eating problem. I’m not trying to lose weight. It’s more like I’m neglecting eating, and my body is showing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you deserve to eat when you're feeling down or sad?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, sometimes. Not in a dramatic way where I think, “I’m not allowed to eat,” exactly, but when I’m really down, there’s this feeling like I don’t deserve much of anything, including taking care of myself.\n\nI keep getting stuck on the idea that I’m just a burden. At work, at the clinic, even at home with my family. Like if I make some small mistake — say a room turnover takes too long, or I forget something minor and someone else has to wait — I’ll replay it in my head like I ruined everyone’s whole day. I know, logically, it might just be a delay or something that happens in a busy place, but it doesn’t feel that way in the moment. It feels like I’ve let everybody down, like I’m making things harder for people who already have enough to deal with.\n\nAnd then when I feel like that, eating feels… I don’t know, almost like something I haven’t earned. Like why am I stopping to feed myself when I’ve already caused problems or made people carry me? I’m not saying that makes sense. If someone else told me that, I’d tell them they absolutely deserve to eat. But when it’s me, when I’m sad or ashamed, it’s harder to believe that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F32.0", "F50.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think it probably would improve some if my mood got better. I don’t think the eating is its own separate thing, exactly. It feels tied into how low and dragged down I’ve been, because when I’m in that place, I just don’t take care of myself properly.

Like what really brought me in is that my eating has gotten so off that some days, before a shift, I barely get down coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already working and I realize that’s basically all I’ve had. It’s not that I’m making some plan to eat less or trying to lose weight. It’s more like eating just falls away when I’m not doing well.

And then my scrubs have been hanging loose, which is kind of embarrassing to admit how much I notice it, but I do. I wear them all the time, so when they start fitting differently, it’s hard to ignore. That’s what made me think, okay, this isn’t just a bad couple days or me being busy. My appetite or my routine or whatever you’d call it has really gotten messed up.

So yes, if the sadness lifted, I think I’d have a better chance of eating normally again. I’d probably be more likely to stop and actually have a meal instead of just coffee and something small and then pushing through. But right now it feels like the low mood makes it really easy to neglect it.
